( H , H 3 ) (H,H^{3}) -smoothing effect and convergence of solutions of stochastic two-dimensional anisotropic Navier-Stokes equations driven by colored noise
Hui Liu, Dong Su, Chengfeng Sun, Jie Xin

Abstract
This paper is devoted to the higher regularity and convergence of solutions of anisotropic Navier-Stokes (NS) equations with additive colored noise and white noise on two-dimensional torus 𝕋 2 \mathbb{T}^{2} . Under the conditions that the external force f ⁡ ( x ) f(\textbf{x}) belongs to the phase space H H and the noise intensity function h ⁡ ( x ) h(\textbf{x}) satisfies ‖ ∇ h ‖ L ∞ ≤ π ​ δ ​ ν ​ λ 1 2 \|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sqrt{\pi\delta}\frac{\nu\lambda_{1}}{2} , it was proved that the random anisotropic NS equations possess a tempered ( H , H 2 ) (H,H^{2}) -random attractor whose (box-counting) fractal dimension in H 2 H^{2} is finite. This was achieved by establishing, first, an H 2 H^{2} bounded absorbing set and, second, an ( H , H 2 ) (H,H^{2}) -smoothing effect of the system which lifts the compactness and finite-dimensionality of the attractor in H H to that in H 2 H^{2} . Since the force f f belongs only to H H , the H 2 H^{2} -regularity of solutions as well as the H 2 H^{2} -bounded absorbing set was constructed by an indirect approach of estimating the H 2 H^{2} -distance between the solution of the random anisotropic NS equations and that of the corresponding deterministic anisotropic NS equations. When the external force f ⁡ ( x ) f(\textbf{x}) belongs to H 2 H^{2} and the noise intensity function h ⁡ ( x ) h(\textbf{x}) satisfies the Assumption 2 , it was proved that the random anisotropic NS equations possess a tempered ( H , H 3 ) (H,H^{3}) -random attractor whose (box-counting) fractal dimension in H 3 H^{3} is finite. Finally, we prove the upper semi-continuity of random attractors and the convergence of solutions of ( 8.3 ) as δ → 0 \delta\rightarrow 0 in the spaces ( H , H ) (H,H) , ( H , H 1 ) (H,H^{1}) , ( H 1 , H 2 ) (H^{1},H^{2}) and ( H 2 , H 3 ) (H^{2},H^{3}) , respectively.

35B40, 35B41, 60H15

Introduction

In this paper we study the asymptotic dynamics of the following stochastic two-dimensional anisotropic Navier-Stokes (NS) equations driven by colored noise on
\mathbb{T}^{2}=[0,L]^{2}
,
L>0
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\partial\textbf{u}_{h}}{\partial t}-\nu\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u_{h}\\
\partial_{xx}v_{h}\end{array}\right)+(\textbf{u}_{h}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{u}_{h}+\nabla p=f(\textbf{x})+h(\textbf{x})\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\\
&\nabla\cdot\textbf{u}_{h}=0,\\
&\textbf{ u}_{h}(0)=\textbf{u}_{h,0},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

endowed with periodic boundary conditions and
t>0
. Here
\textbf{u}_{h}=(u_{h},v_{h})
is the velocity field of fluid and
\textbf{x}=(x,y)
,
\nu>0
is the kinematic viscosity,
p
is the scalar pressure and
f\in H
represents volume forces that are applied to the fluid, where
H=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}:\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u\,{\rm{d}}x=0,\,{\rm div}\,u=0\right\}
is the phase space of the system.
\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)
is the colored noise with correlation time
\delta>0
.
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with
\mathcal{F}
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra induced by the compact open topology of
\Omega
, and
\mathbb{P}
the corresponding Wiener measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. The subscript
``_{h}"
indicates the dependence on the noise intensity
h(\textbf{x})
.
When
h\equiv 0
, we introduce the deterministic anisotropic NS equations on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{\partial\textbf{u}}{\partial t}-\nu\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u\\
\partial_{xx}v\end{array}\right)+(\textbf{u}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{u}+\nabla p=f(\textbf{x}),\\
&\nabla\cdot\textbf{u}=0,\\
&\textbf{u}(0)=\textbf{u}_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.2)

The anisotropic NS equations are fundamental mathematical models in fluid mechanics, see, e.g.
[

,

,

,

,

]
. Meanwhile, asymptotic behavior of the non-autonomous NS equations has been investigated in

.

studied the regularity of random attractors of the stochastic NS equations. Inspired by
[

,
51
]
and the similar method, if
f\in H^{s}
,
s>0
, the regularity of the global attractor of the system (
1.2
) has been proved in
H^{s}
, and then the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
of the system (
1.2
) is bounded in
H^{s+1}
.
[

]
proved the

-boundedness of the pullback attractors for non-autonomous two-dimensional NS equations, and then we will prove the

Recently, the anisotropic fluid equations have been investigated by many authors in

By the Proposition 12.4 in

, we can deduce that the global attractor

of the two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
) is a global
(H,H^{1})-
attractor. That is to say, the global attractor

is a compact set in

and attracts every bounded set in

under the norm of
H^{1}
. The

are smooth solutions, see, e.g.

, up to now the attractor was not obtained to be
(H,H^{2})
and

Recently, under general settings that the

-attractor of the two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations has been investigated. By using the asymptotic compactness,

-type attractors of the other fluids equations have been investigated in

By proving the

, under certain conditions that the random dynamical system generated by (
1.1
) has an
(H,H^{2})
-random attractor, and, in addition, this random attractor has finite (box-counting) fractal dimension in

. Since the idea of estimating

does not apply to stochastic equations, the
H^{2}
-random absorbing sets need be constructed differently here from
[

. when

Main results. Main techniques

In this subsection, we introduce some main results and main techniques as follows. By virtue of the
[
16
]
, we reformulated the stochastic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations via a scalar Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
as the following random equation

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}\textbf{v}}{{\rm{d}}t}+\nu A_{1}\textbf{v}+B\big(\textbf{v}(t)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\big)=f(\textbf{x})-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),

(1.3)

where
A_{1}\textbf{u}=-P\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u\\
\partial_{xx}v\end{array}\right)=-\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u\\
\partial_{xx}v\end{array}\right)
,
B(u)=P((u\cdot\nabla)u)
with
P:\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}\to H
the Helmholtz-Leray orthogonal projection, and
y_{\delta}
is a tempered random variable. The intensity function of the noise
h(\textbf{x})
is supposed to satisfy the following assumption. In this paper,
C
is a positive constant whose value may vary from line to line and even in the same line.

Assumption 1
.

Let
h\in H^{3}
, and
\nabla\cdot h=0
and

\displaystyle\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}<\sqrt{\pi\delta}\frac{1}{2}\nu\lambda_{1},

where
\lambda_{1}=4\pi^{2}/L^{2}
is the first eigenvalue of the Stokes operator
A
.

In order to present the main results in the Section
7
, we need to introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 2
.

Let
f\in H^{2}
,
h\in H^{4}
and
\nabla\cdot h=0
and

\displaystyle\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}<\sqrt{\pi\delta}\frac{1}{2}\nu\lambda_{1},

where
\lambda_{1}=4\pi^{2}/L^{2}
is the first eigenvalue of the Stokes operator
A
.

In Theorem
4.4
of the Section
4
, we will introduce the following an
H^{2}
-random absorbing set.

Main Theorem 1
.

[
H^{2}
absorbing set]
Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
. The RDS
\phi
generated by the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise has a random absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}_{H^{2}}
, defined as a random
H^{2}
neighborhood of the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
of the deterministic anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
):

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{H^{2}}(\omega)=\left\{\textbf{v}\in H^{2}:\,{\rm dist}_{H^{2}}(\textbf{v},\mathcal{A}_{0})\leq\sqrt{\rho(\omega)}\,\right\},\quad\omega\in\Omega,

where
\rho(\cdot)
is the tempered random variable defined by (
4.26
). As a consequence, the
\mathcal{D}_{H}
-random attractor
\mathcal{A}
of (
1.3
) is a bounded and tempered random set in
H^{2}
.

Since the idea of estimating
u_{t}
does not apply to random equations (
1.3
), the
H^{2}
-random absorbing sets need be constructed differently, see the Section
4
. When
f
belongs only to
H
, we can not prove the estimation the
H^{2}
norm of
v
of the random equations (
1.3
).

In order to prove the Main Theorem
1
, we used a comparison approach to prove the difference
w:=\textbf{v}-\textbf{u}
between the solutions of the random and the deterministic anisotropic NS equations instead of estimating the solution
v
itself, where
u
is taken as a solution corresponding to an initial value lying in the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. By Lemma
4.3
, then we obtained that
w
is asymptotically bounded in
H^{2}
. Inspired by the Proposition 12.4 in
[
50
]
and
\cup_{t\geq 0}u(t)\subset\mathcal{A}_{0}
bounded in
H^{2}
, the
H^{2}
random absorbing set of
v
is proved.

In Section
6
, by Theorem
6.1
, we will show that the random attractor
\mathcal{A}

is not only compact but also finite-dimensional in
H^{2}
, and the attraction happens in
H^{2}
as well. The second Main Theorem is stated as the following.

Main Theorem 2
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
. Then the RDS
\phi
generated by the the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise has a tempered
(H,H^{2})
-random attractor
\mathcal{A}
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}
has finite fractal dimension in
H^{2}
.

By the standard bi-spatial random attractor theory
[
20
]
and the
(H,H^{2})
-smoothing property of the system (
1.3
), we proved the Main Theorem
2
. The smoothing property is essentially a local
(H,H^{2})
-Lipschitz continuity in initial values. Moreover, we obtained the finite-dimensionality of the attractor in
H^{2}
. Therefore, in Theorem
5.6
, we obtained an
(H,H^{2})
-smoothing property as stated below.

Main Theorem 3

(
(H,H^{2})
-smoothing)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
. For any tempered set
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{H}
, there
are random variables
T_{{\mathfrak{D}}}(\cdot)
and
L_{\mathfrak{D}}(\cdot)
such that any two solutions
\textbf{v}_{1}
and
\textbf{v}_{2}
of the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise corresponding to initial values
\textbf{v}_{1,0},

\textbf{v}_{2,0}
in
\mathfrak{D}\left(\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega)}\omega\right)
, respectively, satisfy

\displaystyle\big\|\textbf{v}_{1}\!\big(T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega),\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega)}\omega,\textbf{v}_{1,0}\big)-\textbf{v}_{2}\big(T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega),\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega)}\omega,\textbf{v}_{2,0}\big)\big\|{{}^{2}_{H^{2}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq L_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega)\|\textbf{v}_{1,0}-\textbf{v}_{2,0}\|^{2},\quad\omega\in\Omega.

(1.4)

In Section
7
, by Theorem
7.5
, we showed that the random attractor
\mathcal{A}

is not only compact but also finite-dimensional in
H^{3}
, and the attraction happens in
H^{3}
as well. The fourth Main Theorem is stated as the following.

Main Theorem 4
.

Let Assumption
2
hold and
f\in H^{2}
. Then the RDS
\phi
generated by the the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise has a tempered
(H,H^{3})
-random attractor
\mathcal{A}
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}
has finite fractal dimension in
H^{3}
.

By the
H^{3}
-absorbing set and the
(H,H^{3})
-smoothing property of the system (
1.3
), we proved the Main Theorem
4
. The smoothing property is essentially a local
(H,H^{3})
-Lipschitz continuity in initial values. Moreover, we obtained the finite-dimensionality of the attractor in
H^{3}
. Therefore, in Theorem
7.5
, we obtained an
(H,H^{3})
-smoothing property as stated below.

Main Theorem 5

(
(H,H^{3})
-smoothing)
.

Let Assumption
2
hold and
f\in H^{2}
. For any tempered set
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{H}
, there
are random variables
T_{{\mathfrak{D}}}^{*}(\cdot)
and
L_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\cdot)
such that any two solutions
\textbf{v}_{1}
and
\textbf{v}_{2}
of the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise corresponding to initial values
\textbf{v}_{1,0},

\textbf{v}_{2,0}
in
\mathfrak{D}\left(\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega)}\omega\right)
, respectively, satisfy

\displaystyle\big\|\textbf{v}_{1}\!\big(T_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega),\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega)}\omega,\textbf{v}_{1,0}\big)-\textbf{v}_{2}\big(T_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega),\theta_{-T_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega)}\omega,\textbf{v}_{2,0}\big)\big\|{{}^{2}_{H^{3}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq L_{\mathfrak{D}}^{*}(\omega)\|\textbf{v}_{1,0}-\textbf{v}_{2,0}\|^{2},\quad\omega\in\Omega.

(1.5)

In Section
8
, we firstly proved the upper semi-continuity of random attractors as
\delta\rightarrow 0
. Finally, we proved the convergence of solutions of (
8.3
) as
\delta\rightarrow 0
in the spaces
(H,H)
,
(H,H^{1})
,

and

, respectively. Then we introduce the main result as stated below.

Main Theorem 6
.

Let Assumption
2
hold and
f\in H^{2}
. There exists a random variable
\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*}
such that any two solutions
\textbf{v}_{1}
and
\textbf{v}_{2}
of the random anisotropic NS equations (
1.3
) driven by colored noise corresponding to initial values
\textbf{v}_{\delta,0},

\textbf{v}_{0}
in
\mathfrak{B}(\theta_{-\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*}})
, respectively, satisfy

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\|\textbf{u}_{\delta}(\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*},\theta_{-\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*}}\omega,\textbf{u}_{\delta,0})-\textbf{u}(\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*},\theta_{-\bar{T}_{\omega}^{*}}\omega,\textbf{u}_{0})\big\|^{2}_{H^{3}}=0,\quad\omega\in\Omega.

Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce the
(X,Y)
-random attractor theory and their fractal dimensions. In Section
3
, we first introduce the functional spaces that are necessary to study two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations, and then associate an RDS to the random two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations. Under new conditions, we prove an
(H,H^{1})
-random attractor of finite fractal dimension in
H
. In Section
4
, by an indirect approach of estimating the
H^{2}
-distance between the random and the deterministic solution trajectories we construct an
H^{2}
random absorbing set. In Section
5
, we show the crucial
(H,H^{2})
-smoothing of the RDS from which it follows in Section
6
that the random attractor in
H
is in fact an
(H,H^{2})
-random attractor of finite fractal dimension in
H^{2}
. In Section
7
, we first show the
(H,H^{3})
-smoothing of the RDS, and then the random attractor in
H
is in fact an
(H,H^{3})
-random attractor of finite fractal dimension in
H^{3}
. In Section
8
, we prove the upper semi-continuity of random attractors and the convergence of solutions of (
8.3
) as
\delta\rightarrow 0
in the spaces
(H,H)
,
(H,H^{1})
,
(H^{1},H^{2})
and
(H^{2},H^{3})
, respectively.

2
Preliminaries:
(X,Y)
-random attractors and their fractal dimension

In this section, we first introduce the basic theory of
(X,Y)
-random attractors and their fractal dimension needed in this paper. The readers are referred to
[
3
,
16
,
20
,
28
,
30
,
36
]
and references therein.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be the standard probability space, where
\Omega=\{\omega\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}):\omega(0)=0\}
, with the open compact topology,
\mathcal{F}
is its Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbb{P}
is the Wiener measure on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. We consider the Wiener shift
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
defined on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
as stated below

\displaystyle\theta_{t}\omega(\cdot)=\omega(t+\cdot)-\omega(t)

for any
\omega\in\Omega
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
denote a Banach space and be a probability space with a group
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of measure-preserving self-transformations on
\Omega
.
Denote by
\mathcal{B}(\cdot)
the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of a metric space, and let

\mathbb{R}^{+}:=[0,\infty)
.

Definition 2.1
.

A mapping
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Omega\times X\rightarrow X
is defined a
random dynamical system (RDS)
in
X
, if

1.

\phi
is
(\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\times\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{B}(X),\mathcal{B}(X))
-measurable;

2.

for all
\omega\in\Omega
,
\phi(0,\omega,\cdot)
is the identity on
X
;

3.

\phi
satisfies the cocycle property as stated below

\displaystyle\phi(t+s,\omega,x)=\phi(t,\theta_{s}\omega,\phi(s,\omega,x)),\quad\forall t,s\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~\omega\in\Omega,~x\in X;

4.

the mapping
x\mapsto\phi(t,\omega,x)
is continuous.

A set-valued mapping
\mathfrak{D}
:
\Omega\mapsto 2^{X}\setminus\emptyset
,
\omega\mapsto\mathfrak{D}(\omega)
, is called a
random set
in
X
if it is
measurable
in the sense that the mapping
\omega\to{\rm dist}_{X}(x,\mathfrak{D}(\omega))
is
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
-measurable for each
x\in X
. If each its image
\mathfrak{D}(\omega)
is closed (or bounded, compact, etc.) in
X
, then
\mathfrak{D}
is called a closed (or bounded, compact, etc.) random set in
X
.

A random variable
\zeta:\Omega\mapsto X
is called
tempered
if

\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\varepsilon t}|\zeta(\theta_{-t}\omega)|=0,\quad\varepsilon>0.

A random set
\mathfrak{D}
in
X
is called
tempered
if there exists a tempered random variable
\zeta
such that
\|\mathfrak{D}(\omega)\|_{X}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{D}(\omega)}\|x\|\leq\zeta(\omega)
for any
\omega\in\Omega
.

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{X}
the collection of all the tempered random sets in
X
.

Definition 2.2
.

A random set
\mathfrak{B}
in
X
is called a
(random) absorbing set
for
\phi
if it pullback absorbs any tempered random set in
X
, i.e., if for every
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
there exists a random variable
T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\cdot)
such that

\displaystyle\phi(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\mathfrak{D}(\theta_{-t}\omega))\subset\mathfrak{B}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{D}}(\omega),\ \omega\in\Omega.

Definition 2.3
.

A random set
\mathcal{A}
is called the
random attractor
for
\phi
in
X
if

1.

\mathcal{A}
is a tempered and compact random set in
X
;

2.

\mathcal{A}
is invariant under
\phi
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\theta_{t}\omega)=\phi(t,\omega,\mathcal{A}(\omega)),\quad t\geq 0,~\omega\in\Omega;

3.

\mathcal{A}
pullback attracts any tempered set in
X
, that is, for all
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
,

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}{\rm dist}_{X}\big(\phi(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\mathfrak{D}(\theta_{-t}\omega)),\,\mathcal{A}(\omega)\big)=0,\quad\omega\in\Omega,

where

{\rm dist}_{X}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Hausdorff semi-metric between subsets of
X
, that is,

{\rm dist}_{X}(A,B)=\sup_{a\in A}\inf_{b\in B}\|a-b\|_{X},\quad\forall A,B\subset X.

For the colored noise, given
\delta>0
, then we introduce one-dimensional stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle{\rm{d}}\zeta_{\delta}+\frac{1}{\delta}\zeta_{\delta}{\rm{d}}t=\frac{1}{\delta}dW,

(2.1)

where
W
is a two-sided real valued Wiener process defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Let
\zeta_{\delta}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a random variable defined by

\displaystyle\zeta_{\delta}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{\frac{s}{\delta}}dW,~~\forall\omega\in\Omega.

By
[
28
,
30
]
, we have that
\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)
is the unique stationary solution of (
2.1
). Then the process
\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)
is defined a real valued colored noise which is a stationary Wiener process with mean zero and variance
\frac{1}{2\delta}
. Moreover, there exists a
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
-invariant subset
\Omega
of full measure, such that for any
\omega\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}y_{\delta}(\theta_{r}\omega){\rm{d}}r=\mathbb{E}[y_{\delta}]=0~~uniformly~~for~~0<\delta\leq 1.

(2.2)

Assume that
Y
is a Banach space with continuous embedding
Y\hookrightarrow X
.

Definition 2.4
.

A random set
\mathcal{A}
is called the
(X,Y)
-random attractor
for
\phi
in
X
, if

1.

\mathcal{A}
is a tempered and compact random set in
Y
;

2.

\mathcal{A}
is invariant under
\phi
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\theta_{t}\omega)=\phi(t,\omega,\mathcal{A}(\omega)),\quad t\geq 0,~\omega\in\Omega;

3.

\mathcal{A}
pullback attracts any tempered set in
X
in the metric of
Y
, that is, for all
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
,

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}{\rm dist}_{Y}\big(\phi(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\mathfrak{D}(\theta_{-t}\omega)),\,\mathcal{A}(\omega)\big)=0,\quad\omega\in\Omega.

Note that in addition to the temperedness, compactness and pullback attraction in
Y
, the measurability in
Y
of an
(X,Y)
-random attractor was also required by definition.

Then we introduce the following criterion as stated below, see, e.g.
[
20
, Theorem 19]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(
[
20
, Theorem 19]
)
Suppose that
\phi
is an RDS. If

1.

\phi
has a random absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}
which is a tempered and closed random set in
Y
;

2.

\phi
is
(X,Y)
-asymptotically compact, that is, for any
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
and
t_{n}\to\infty
, any sequence

\{\phi(t_{n},\theta_{-t_{n}}\omega,x_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
x_{n}\in\mathfrak{D}(\theta_{-t_{n}}\omega)
,
\omega\in\Omega
, has a convergent subsequence in
Y
,

then
\phi
has a unique
(X,Y)
-random attractor
\mathcal{A}
defined by

\mathcal{A}(\omega)=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\overline{\bigcup_{t\geq s}\phi(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\mathfrak{B}(\theta_{-t}\omega))}^{Y},\quad\omega\in\Omega.

Let
E
be a precompact set in
X
, and suppose that
N(E,\varepsilon)
is the minimum number of balls of radius
\varepsilon
in
X
required to cover
E
. Then the
(box-counting) fractal dimension of
E
is given by

d_{f}^{X}(E)=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\log N(E,\varepsilon)}{-\log\varepsilon},

where the superscript “
\ {}^{X}
” indicates the space to which the fractal dimension is referred.

3
The anisotropic Navier-Stokes equations and the attractors in
H

In this section, we first introduce the basic of functional spaces. As
f
belongs only to
H
, we can prove that the global attractor of the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
) is
(H,H^{2})
-attractor. When
f\in H^{2}
, we also can prove that the global attractor of the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
) is
(H,H^{3})
-attractor. We transform the stochastic anisotropic NS equations (
3.4
) to a random anisotropic NS equations which generate an RDS
\phi
on
H
. Finally, under new conditions, we will prove an
(H,H^{1})
-random attractor of finite fractal dimension in
H
.

3.1
Functional spaces

In this subsection, we first introduce Sobolev spaces of periodic functions to study the periodic boundary conditions. The following settings are standard, the readers are referred to, e.g.,
[
4
,
50
,
55
,
56
]
. etc.

Let
(C_{per}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
be the space of two-component infinitely differentiable functions that are
L
-periodic in each direction, and by
\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}
,
\mathbb{H}_{per}^{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{per}^{2}
the completion of
(C_{per}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
with respect to the
(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
,
(H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
and
(H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
norm, respectively.
\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}_{per}^{1}
are endowed with the inner products as follows

\displaystyle(u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u\cdot v{\rm{d}}x,~~u,v\in\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2},

\displaystyle((u,v))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\nabla u_{j}\cdot\nabla v_{j}{\rm{d}}x,~~u,v\in\mathbb{H}_{per}^{1}.

The phase space
(H,\|\cdot\|)
denotes a subspace of
\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}
of functions with zero mean and free divergence, that is,

\displaystyle H=\left\{u\in\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}:\,\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u\ {\rm{d}}x=0,\ {\rm div}\,u=0\right\},

endowed with the norm of
\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}
, i.e.,

\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}}
. For
p>2
, we write
(L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))^{2}
simply as
L^{p}
and its norm as
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}}
.

Note that any
u\in\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}
has an expression
u=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\hat{u}_{j}e^{{\rm i}j\cdot x}
, where
{\rm i}=\sqrt{-1}
and
\hat{u}_{j}
are Fourier coefficients. Hence,
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u\,{\rm{d}}x=0
is equivalent to
\hat{u}_{0}=0
. For any
u\in H
, we have

u=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}}\hat{u}_{j}e^{{\rm i}j\cdot x}.

Suppose that
P:\mathbb{L}_{per}^{2}\rightarrow H
is the Helmholtz-Leray orthogonal projection operator. In this periodic space, we define the Stokes operator
A\textbf{u}=-P\Delta\textbf{u}==-\Delta\textbf{u}
for any
\textbf{u}\in D(A)
. Then we set
A_{1}\textbf{u}=-P\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u\\
\partial_{xx}v\end{array}\right)=-\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{yy}u\\
\partial_{xx}v\end{array}\right)
. In this bounded domain, the projector
P
does not commute with derivatives. The operator
A^{-1}
is a self-adjoint positive-definite compact operator from
H
to
H
.

For
s>0
, the Sobolev space
H^{s}:=D(A^{s/2})
is given by as stated below

H^{s}=\left\{u\in H:\|u\|^{2}_{H^{s}}=\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\backslash\{0\}}|j|^{2s}|\hat{u}_{j}|^{2}<\infty\right\},

endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}=\|A^{s/2}\cdot\|
. Then the dual space of
H^{s}
is defined by
H^{-s}
.

For any
u,v\in H^{1}
, we define the bilinear form

B(u,v)=P((u\cdot\nabla)v),

and, in particular,
B(u):=B(u,u)
. For any
u,v,w\in H^{1}
, we set

\displaystyle\langle B(u,v),w\rangle=b(u,v,w)=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}u_{i}\frac{\partial v_{j}}{\partial x_{i}}w_{j}{\rm{d}}x.

Then we introduce the Poincaré’s inequality as stated below, see, e.g.,
[
50
, lemma 5.40]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
u\in H^{1}
, then we have

\|u\|\leq\left(\frac{L}{2\pi}\right)\|u\|_{H^{1}}.

(3.1)

Thus, let

\lambda_{1}:=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{L^{2}},

we can deduce that
\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\geq\lambda_{1}\|u\|^{2}
. For later purpose we let
\lambda:=\alpha\nu\lambda_{1}/4
.

Assume that the noise intensity
h
satisfy Assumption
1
, that is,
h\in H^{3}
and

\displaystyle\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}<\sqrt{\pi\delta}\frac{1}{2}\nu\lambda_{1}.

(3.2)

(In particular,
\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}<\sqrt{\pi\delta}\frac{\nu}{2}
for the scale
L=2\pi
).
Clearly, the tolerance of the noise intensity increases as the kinematic viscosity
\nu
increases.

Let
f\in H
,
\textbf{u}_{h}(0)\in H
and
\omega\in\Omega
, we say that a mapping
\textbf{u}(\cdot,\omega,\textbf{u}_{h}(0)):[0,+\infty)\rightarrow H
is defined a solution of system (
1.1
) if for any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\textbf{u}(\cdot,\omega,\textbf{u}_{h}(0))\in C([0,+\infty);H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})

and
u
satisfies

\displaystyle(\textbf{u}_{h}(t),\xi)

\displaystyle+\nu\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{y}u_{h},\partial_{y}\xi_{1}){\rm{d}}s+\nu\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{x}v_{h},\partial_{x}\xi_{2}){\rm{d}}s+\int_{0}^{t}b(\textbf{u}_{h},\textbf{u}_{h},\xi){\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle=(\textbf{u}_{0},\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}(f(\textbf{x}),\xi){\rm{d}}s+\int_{0}^{t}(h(\textbf{x})\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega),\xi){\rm{d}}s

(3.3)

for any
t>0
and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in H^{1}
. Then the system (
1.1
) is rewritten as follows

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}\textbf{u}_{h}}{{\rm{d}}t}+\nu A_{1}\textbf{u}_{h}+B(\textbf{u}_{h},\textbf{u}_{h})=f+h\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),

(3.4)

in
H^{-1}
.In
[
55
,
56
]
, applying the Galerkin method, we say that for any
\textbf{u}_{h}(0)\in H
and
\omega\in\Omega
, the system (
1.1
) has a unique solution
\textbf{u}_{h}
in the sense of (
3.1
).

3.2
Global attractor of the deterministic anisotropic Navier-Stokes equations

In this subsection, we recall the global attractor of the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
), i.e. for
h(\textbf{x})\equiv 0
, which will be crucial for our analysis later. Under projection
P
, the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
1.2
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\textbf{u}+\nu A_{1}\textbf{u}+B(\textbf{u})=f,\quad u(0)=u_{0}.

(3.5)

The system (
3.5
) generates an autonomous dynamical system
S
in phase space
H
with a global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Inspired by the Proposition 12.4 in
[
50
]
, the following regularity result of the attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
seems optimal for
f\in H
in the literature.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
f\in H
, the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) generate a semigroup
S
which possesses a global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
in
H
and has an
absorbing set which is bounded in
H^{2}
. In particular, the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is bounded in
H^{2}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
f\in H^{2}
, the deterministic two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) generate a semigroup
S
which possesses a global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
in
H
and has an
absorbing set which is bounded in
H^{3}
. In particular, the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is bounded in
H^{3}
.

For
f\in H
, we will show that the global attractor
\mathcal{A}
is not only bounded in
H^{2}
, but also has finite fractal dimension (and thus compact) in
H^{2}
. Moreover, For
f\in H^{2}
, we also will show that the global attractor
\mathcal{A}
is not only bounded in
H^{3}
, but also has finite fractal dimension in
H^{3}
.

Remark 3.4
.

When
f
belongs only to
H
, we cannot deduce an energy equation of
\|Au\|
directly and thus Lemma
3.3
was proved by estimating the time-derivatives
\partial_{t}\textbf{u}
of solutions rather than estimating the solutions
u
themselves. However, this method does not apply to the random two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) since the colored noise is not derivable in time. Therefore, in Section
4
we will employ a comparison approach to prove
H^{2}
random absorbing sets for (
3.17
). When
f\in H^{2}
, we can deduce an energy equation of
\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}u\|
directly. And then this method apply to the random two-dimensional anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) to prove random absorbing set in
H^{3}
.

3.3
Generation of an RDS

For the colored noise
\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)
and the Wiener process
W(t,\omega)=\omega(t)
, we recall some main results as follows, see, e.g.,
[
28
,
31
]

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume the correlation time
\delta\in(0,1]
. Then there is a
\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
invariant subset
\Omega
of full measure,such that for
\omega\in\Omega
,

(i)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\frac{\omega(t)}{t}=0;

(ii) the mapping

\displaystyle(t,\omega)\mapsto\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)=-\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int^{0}_{-\infty}e^{\frac{s}{\delta}}\theta_{t}\omega(s){\rm{d}}s

denotes a stationary solution (also called a colored noise) of the one-dimensional stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle{\rm{d}}\zeta_{\delta}+\frac{1}{\delta}\zeta_{\delta}{\rm{d}}t=\frac{1}{\delta}dW

with continuous trajectories satisfying

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\frac{|\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|}{t}=0,~~for~~any~~0<\delta\leq 1.

(3.6)

In order to transform (
3.4
), we introduce the following random equation driven by colored noise,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}y_{\delta}}{{\rm{d}}t}=-y_{\delta}+\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega).

(3.7)

By virtue of (
3.6
), we can get that for any
\omega\in\Omega
, the integral

\displaystyle y_{\delta}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{s}\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega){\rm{d}}s

(3.8)

is convergent. Meanwhile,
y_{\delta}(\omega)
denotes a well defined random variable. By
[
15
,
16
]
, we have that
z(\theta_{t}\omega)
is the stationary solution of the one-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck. Then there exists a
\theta_{t}
-invariant subset
\tilde{\Omega}\subset\Omega
of full measure such that
z(\theta_{t}\omega)
is continuous in
t
for any
\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}
and the random variable
|z(\cdot)|
is tempered,

By virtue of the Lemma 3.2 in
[
28
]
and (
2.2
), we introduce the following main proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

Assume that
y_{\delta}
is the random variable as (
3.8
), we have that the mapping

\displaystyle(t,\omega)\mapsto y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)=e^{-t}\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{s}\zeta_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega){\rm{d}}s

(3.9)

is a stationary solution of (
3.7
) with continuous trajectories. Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}(y_{\delta})=0
and for any
\omega\in\Omega
and
T>0
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)=z(\theta_{t}\omega)~~uniformly~~on~~[0,T],

(3.10)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|}{|t|}=0~~uniformly~~for~~0<\delta\leq\frac{1}{2},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}y_{\delta}(\theta_{r}\omega){\rm{d}}r=0~~uniformly~~for~~0<\delta\leq\frac{1}{2},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}(|y_{\delta}(\omega)|)=\mathbb{E}(|z(\omega)|).

(3.13)

In
[
3
,
65
]
, for any given
\delta>0
, then we get

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{m}\,{\rm{d}}s=\mathbb{E}\big(|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{m}\big)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1+m}{2})}{\sqrt{\pi\delta^{m}}},\quad\omega\in\tilde{\Omega},

(3.14)

for all
m\geq 1
, where
\Gamma
is a Gamma function. Hence, we will not distinguish
\tilde{\Omega}
and
\Omega
.

Then we introduce the following a new variable

\displaystyle\textbf{u}_{h}(t)=\textbf{v}(t)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\quad t>0,\ \omega\in\Omega,

(3.15)

here,
\textbf{v}(t)=(v_{1},v_{2})
and
0=\nabla\cdot\textbf{u}_{h}=\nabla\cdot(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))=\nabla\cdot\textbf{v}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla\textbf{v}\|^{2}=\|\nabla\times\textbf{v}\|^{2}\leq 2\|(\partial_{y}v_{1},\partial_{x}v_{2})\|^{2}.

(3.16)

By (
3.4
) and (
3.15
), we have the following abstract evolution equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{{\rm{d}}\textbf{v}}{{\rm{d}}t}+\nu A_{1}\textbf{v}+B\big(\textbf{v}(t)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\big)=f(\textbf{x})-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\\
&\textbf{v}(0)=\textbf{u}_{h}(0)-hy_{\delta}(\omega).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.17)

By the classic Galerkin method in
[
3
,
55
]
, the system (
3.17
) has a unique weak solution as stated below.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
f\in H
and Assumption
1
hold. For any
\textbf{v}(0)\in H
and
\omega\in\Omega
, then there exists a unique weak solution

\displaystyle\textbf{v}(t)\in C_{loc}([0,\infty);H)\cap L^{2}_{loc}(0,\infty;H^{1})

satisfying (
3.17
) in distribution sense with
\textbf{v}|_{t=0}=\textbf{v}(0)
. In addition, this solution is continuous in initial data
\textbf{v}(0)
in
H
.

Therefore, by

\phi(t,\omega,v_{0})=\textbf{v}(t,\omega,v_{0}),\quad\forall t\geq 0,\,\omega\in\Omega,\,v_{0}\in H,

where
v
is the solution of (
3.17
),
we associated an RDS
\phi
in
H
to (
3.17
).

3.4
The random attractor in
H

In this subsection, we first will introduce the following two basic lemmas, see, e.g.,
[
55
,
56
]
.

Lemma 3.8

(Gronwall’s lemma)
.

Assume that
x(t)
is a function from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

\dot{x}+a(t)x\leq b(t).

Then for any
t\geq s
,

x(t)\leq e^{-\int^{t}_{s}a(\tau)\,{\rm{d}}\tau}x(s)+\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\int^{t}_{\eta}a(\tau)\,{\rm{d}}\tau}b(\eta)\,{\rm{d}}\eta.

Lemma 3.9

(Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality)
.

If
u\in L^{q}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
D^{m}u\in L^{r}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
1\leq q,r\leq\infty
, then there exists a positive constant
C
such that

\|D^{j}u\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|D^{m}u\|_{L^{r}}^{a}\|u\|^{1-a}_{L^{q}},

where

\frac{1}{p}-\frac{j}{2}=a\left(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{m}{2}\right)+\frac{1-a}{q},\quad 1\leq p\leq\infty,~0\leq j\leq m,~\frac{j}{m}\leq a\leq 1,

and

C
depends only on
\{m,\,j,\,a,\,q,\,r\}
.

Lemma 3.10

(
H
bound)
.

Let
f\in H
and Assumption
1
hold.
There exist random variables
T_{1}(\omega)
and
\zeta_{1}(\omega)
such that any
solution
v
of the system (
3.17
) corresponding to initial values
\textbf{v}(0)\in H
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega).

Proof.

By (
3.2
),
{\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}}/\sqrt{\pi\delta}<\frac{\nu\lambda_{1}}{2}
, then there exist
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\beta>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}}=(\frac{1}{2}-\alpha)\nu\lambda_{1},

(3.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}}\left(1+\beta\right)=(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\alpha)\nu\lambda_{1}.

(3.19)

Taking the inner product of the first equation (
3.17
) with
v
in
H
, by integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+2\nu\|\partial_{y}v_{1}\|^{2}+2\nu\|\partial_{x}v_{2}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-2(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v})

\displaystyle+2\big(f-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}\big),

(3.20)

by (
3.16
) and Poincaré’s inequality (
3.1
), it yields

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\nu\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\big|\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v }\big)\big|+2\big|\big(f-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}\big)\big|.

(3.21)

Applying the divergence free conditions and the Hölder inequality, Young’s inequality and Poincaré’s inequality, we can deduce that

\displaystyle 2\big|\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v}\big)\big|

\displaystyle=2\big|\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v}\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\big|\big(B(\textbf{v},hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v}\big)\big|+2\big|\big(B(hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\textbf{v}\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\textbf{v}|^{2}|\nabla h|\ {\rm{d}}x+2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}|\textbf{v}||h||\nabla h|\ {\rm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq 2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\textbf{v}\|\|h\|

\displaystyle\leq 2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu\lambda_{1}}{8}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|h\|^{2},

(3.22)

and

\displaystyle 2\big|\big(f-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}\big)\big|\leq\frac{\alpha\nu\lambda_{1}}{8}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\|f\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\left(\|Ah\|^{2}+\|h\|^{2}\right).

(3.23)

Inserting (
3.4
) and (
3.23
) into (
3.21
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|h\|^{2}

\displaystyle+C\|f\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\left(\|h\|^{2}+\|Ah\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right),

and then,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\left[\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}-2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\right]\|\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}\leq C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right).

By Gronwall’s lemma, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{t}|z(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}t+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}|z(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{t}|z(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|z(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s,

and then

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}t+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}|z(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{t}|z(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|z(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s.

(3.24)

Applying (
3.14
) for
m=1
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\pm\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}s=\mathbb{E}(|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}},\quad\omega\in\Omega,

so there exists a random variable
T_{1}(\omega)\geq 1
such that, for every
t\geq T_{1}(\omega)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}s\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}}+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}},

and then

\displaystyle 2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\sqrt{\pi\delta}}\left(1+\beta\right)t

\displaystyle=\left(1-\alpha\right)\nu\lambda_{1}t\quad\text{(by \eqref{c2})}.

(3.25)

Then by (
3.24
) and (
3.25
), it is easy to deduce that

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}\nu\lambda_{1}t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(1-\frac{3\alpha}{4}\right)\nu\lambda_{1}(s-t)+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s.

Let
\lambda:=\alpha\nu\lambda_{1}/4,

this estimate is then rewritten as

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{t}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s.

Replacing
\omega
with
\theta_{-t}\omega
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+C\int^{0}_{-t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda s+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s.

The random variable is given by

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(\omega):=C\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda s+2\|\nabla h\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{s}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}s,\quad\omega\in\Omega.

Then
\zeta_{1}(\cdot)
is a tempered random variable such that
\zeta_{1}(\omega)\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\|\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega).

(3.26)

This completes the proof of Lemma
3.10
.
∎

Lemma 3.11

(
H^{1}
bound)
.

Let
f\in H
and Assumption
1
hold.
Then for any bounded set
B
in
H
there exists a random variable
T_{B}(\omega)>T_{1}(\omega)
such that, for any
t\geq T_{B}(\omega)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\textbf{v}(0)\in B}\left(\big\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\big\|^{2}+\int_{t-\frac{1}{2}}^{t}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\ {\rm{d}}s\right)\leq\zeta_{2}(\omega),

where
\zeta_{2}(\omega)
is a tempered random variable given by (
3.34
) such that
\zeta_{2}(\omega)>\zeta_{1}(\omega)\geq 1
,
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of the first equation (
3.17
) with
A\textbf{v}
in
H
, by integration by parts we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}v_{1}\Delta v_{1}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}v_{2}\Delta v_{2}dx

\displaystyle=-\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\,A\textbf{v}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\ +\big(f-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),A\textbf{v}\big),

(3.27)

here by direct computations, it yields

\displaystyle\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}v_{1}\Delta v_{1}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}v_{1}\partial_{yy}v_{1}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xy}v_{1}\partial_{xy}v_{1}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{2})^{2}dx.

(3.28)

Similarly, it yields

\displaystyle\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}v_{2}\Delta v_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{2})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}v_{2}\partial_{yy}v_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{2})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xy}v_{2}\partial_{xy}v_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{2})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{1})^{2}dx.

(3.29)

Combining (
3.4
) and (
3.4
) yields

\displaystyle\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}v_{1}\Delta v_{1}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}v_{2}\Delta v_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{2})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{yy}v_{2})^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{1}+\partial_{yy}v_{1})^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}v_{2}+\partial_{yy}v_{2})^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nu\|\Delta\textbf{v}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\nu\|A\textbf{v}\|^{2}.

(3.30)

By the Hölder inequality, the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, Poincaré’s inequality and the Young inequality, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\big|\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),\,A\textbf{v }\big)\big|=\big|\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{4}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|\|A(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{3}{2}}\|A(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{\frac{3}{2}}\right)\left(\|A\textbf{v}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{8}\|A\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+\|Ah\|^{2}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{6}\right),

(3.31)

and

\displaystyle\big(f-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),A\textbf{v}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{8}\|A\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\|f\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}.

(3.32)

Inserting (
3.4
), (
3.4
) and (
3.32
) into (
3.4
), it is easy to get

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\|A\textbf{v}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\|Ah\|^{2}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{6}+\|f\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{6}\right).

(3.33)

Applying Gronwall’s lemma on
(\eta,t)
with
\eta\in(t-1,t)
,
t>1
, it yields

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{\eta}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{\int_{\eta}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{2}+C\int_{\eta}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s,

and then integrating over
\eta\in(t-1,t-\frac{1}{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{4}\int_{t-\frac{1}{2}}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{t-1}^{t-\frac{1}{2}}e^{\int_{\eta}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\eta

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{t-1}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}C(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ce^{C\int_{t-1}^{t}(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2})\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left[\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\eta+\int_{t-1}^{t}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s\right].

Replacing
\omega
with
\theta_{-t}\omega
we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{t-\frac{1}{2}}^{t}e^{C(t-s)+\int_{s-t}^{0}C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ce^{C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left[\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s+\int_{-1}^{0}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s\right].

By (
3.26
),

\displaystyle\nu\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq\nu e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda}\int_{t-1}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda}\left(e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\right),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega),

so
we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{t-\frac{1}{2}}^{t}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{t-\frac{1}{2}}^{t}e^{C(t-s)+\int_{s-t}^{0}C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq Ce^{C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)+\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\,{\rm{d}}s\right),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega),

where we have used the fact that
\zeta_{1}(\omega)\geq 1
.
Hence, let

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}(\omega):=Ce^{C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}\tau}\left(\zeta_{1}(\omega)+\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\ {\rm{d}}s\right),\quad\omega\in\Omega,

(3.34)

here,
\zeta_{2}(\omega)
is a tempered random variable. This completes the proof of Lemma
3.11
.

∎

Let a random set

\mathfrak{B}=\{\mathfrak{B}(\omega)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
in
H^{1}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}(\omega):=\left\{\textbf{v}\in H^{1}:\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}\leq\zeta_{2}(\omega)\right\},\quad\omega\in\Omega,

(3.35)

where
\zeta_{2}(\cdot)
is the tempered random variable given by (
3.34
). By Lemma
3.11
, we have that
\mathfrak{B}
is a random absorbing set of the RDS
\phi
generated by the system (
3.17
). In addition, the compact embedding
H^{1}\hookrightarrow H
gives the compactness of
\mathfrak{B}(\omega)
in
H
, hence the RDS
\phi
has a random attractor
\mathcal{A}
in
H
. Moreover, by the Langa & Robinson
[
36
]
, this random attractor has finite fractal dimension in
H
. Then we introduce the following main result.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
. The RDS
\phi
generated by the random anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) driven by colored noise has a random absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}
which is a tempered and bounded random set in
H^{1}
, and has also a random attractor
\mathcal{A}
in
H
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}
has finite fractal dimension in
H
:

d_{f}^{H}(\mathcal{A}(\omega))\leq d,\quad\omega\in\Omega,

for some positive constant
d
.

In the Section
4
, Section
5
and Section
6
, we will prove that this random attractor
\mathcal{A}
is in fact an
(H,H^{2})
-random attractor of
\phi
.

4
Construction of an
H^{2}
random absorbing set

In this section, we will prove an
H^{2}
random absorbing set of system (
3.17
) as
f\in H
. This will be done by estimating the difference between the solutions of the random anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) and that of the deterministic anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) within the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, see Remark
3.4
. This comparison approach seems first employed in Cui & Li
[
21
]
.

4.1
H^{2}
-distance between random and deterministic trajectories

By the Theorem
3.12
, we have that the random anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) driven by colored noise has an
H^{1}
random absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}
. Meanwhile, by the Lemma
3.3
, we have that the deterministic anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) has a global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
bounded in
H^{2}
, it suffices to restrict ourselves to the random absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}
and the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Note that the
\omega
-dependence of each random time in the following estimates will be crucial for later analysis.

Lemma 4.1

(
H^{1}
-distance)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
. There exist random variables
T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\cdot)
and
\zeta_{4}(\cdot)
, where
\zeta_{4}(\cdot)
is tempered, such that the solutions
v
of the random anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) driven by colored noise and
u
of the deterministic anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) satisfy

\displaystyle\big\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))-A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}(t,\textbf{u}(0))\big\|^{2}\leq\zeta_{4}(\omega),\quad\forall t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega),

for any
\textbf{v}(0)\in\mathfrak{B}(\theta_{-t}\omega)

and
\textbf{u}(0)\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
for
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
w=\textbf{v}-\textbf{u}
denote the difference between the two solutions, here
w=(w_{1},w_{1})
. Then we introduce the following system

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}w}{{\rm{d}}t}+\nu A_{1}w+B(\textbf{v}+hz(\theta_{t}\omega))-B(\textbf{u})=hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega).

(4.1)

Taking the inner product of the system (
4.1
) with
Aw
in
H
, by integration by parts we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}w_{1}\Delta w_{1}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}w_{2}\Delta w_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\big(hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),Aw\big)-\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))-B(\textbf{u}),Aw\big)

\displaystyle=\big(hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),Aw\big)-\big(B(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{u}),Aw\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\big(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),Aw\big)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}(t)+I_{2}(t)+I_{3}(t).

(4.2)

By direct computations and (
3.4
), (
3.4
) and (
3.4
), it yields

\displaystyle\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}w_{1}\Delta w_{1}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}w_{2}\Delta w_{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}w_{1}+\partial_{yy}w_{1})^{2}dx+\frac{1}{2}\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{xx}w_{2}+\partial_{yy}w_{2})^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nu\|\Delta w\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\nu\|Aw\|^{2}.

(4.3)

For three terms of
I_{1}(t)
-
I_{3}(t)
, by Hölder’s inequality, the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, Poincaré’s inequality and Young’s inequality, we obtain the estimates

\displaystyle I_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\nu\|A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|Aw\|+\|hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{8}\|Aw\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2},

(4.4)

\displaystyle I_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\|\left(\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\|^{\frac{3}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\|^{\frac{3}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|\|Aw\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|Aw\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|^{4}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|^{2}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|^{4}\right)|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2},

(4.5)

and

\displaystyle I_{3}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{4}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|_{L^{4}}\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\textbf{v}\|_{L^{4}}+\|hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{4}}\right)\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|_{L^{4}}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{4}}\right)\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Aw\|^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Aw\|^{\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|Aw\|+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|Aw\|^{2}+C\!\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\!\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right).

Since
u
is a trajectory within the global attractor
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we deduce
\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}(t)\|\leq C
for any
t\geq 0
, it yields

\displaystyle I_{2}(t)\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|Aw\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}.

(4.6)

Inserting (
4.1
)-(
4.6
) into (
4.1
) it is easy to deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right).

(4.7)

For any
t>1
and
s\in[t-1,t]
, we apply Gronwall’s lemma to (
4.7
) yields

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\int_{s}^{t}C(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(s)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\int_{s}^{t}e^{\int_{\tau}^{t}C(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\tau)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\int_{s}^{t}C(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left[\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(s)\|^{2}+\int_{t-1}^{t}\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}\tau\right].

Integrating w.r.t
.

s
over
[t-1,t]
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\int_{t-1}^{t}C(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left[\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s+\int_{t-1}^{t}\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}\tau\right].

Since
u
is a trajectory within the global attractor,
\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|^{2}
is uniformly bounded. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(s)\|^{2}=\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(\textbf{v}-\textbf{u})(s)\|^{2}\leq\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{4}+C,

and then

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t)\|^{2}\leq Ce^{\int_{t-1}^{t}C(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}\int_{t-1}^{t}\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\tau)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{4}\right){\rm{d}}\tau.

Replacing
\omega
with
\theta_{-t}\omega
, for any
t>1
it can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,w(0))\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{\int_{t-1}^{t}C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{4}{\rm{d}}\eta+C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4}\,{\rm{d}}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{4}\,{\rm{d}}s+\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\,{\rm{d}}s+1\right).

(4.8)

Recall from (
3.4
) that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\|A\textbf{v}\|^{2}\leq C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{6}\right).

(4.9)

For any
t>3
, integrating both sides of (
4.9
) over
(\tau,t)
with
\tau\in(t-3,t-2)
, then we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}-\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\tau)\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int^{t}_{t-2}\|A\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int^{t}_{t-3}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s+C\int^{t}_{t-3}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s,

and then integrating over
\tau\in(t-3,t-2)
yields

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t)\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int^{t}_{t-2}\|A\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int^{t}_{t-3}\left(|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{2}+1\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s)\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad+C\int^{t}_{t-3}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s,\quad t>3.

For
\varepsilon\in[0,2]
, we replace
\omega
with
\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega
to deduce

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{t-2}^{t}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int^{t}_{t-3}\!\left(|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s-t-\varepsilon}\omega)|^{2}+1\right)\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s+C\int^{-\varepsilon}_{-\varepsilon-3}\!\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}+1\right)\int^{t+\varepsilon}_{t+\varepsilon-5}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int^{0}_{-5}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s,\quad t>5.

(4.10)

By (
3.26
), it yields

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\nu}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega),

where
\zeta_{1}(\omega)\geq 1
is a tempered random variable,
so we can deduce that

\displaystyle\int^{t+\varepsilon}_{t+\varepsilon-5}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-(t+\varepsilon)}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq C\int^{t+\varepsilon}_{t+\varepsilon-5}e^{\left(\frac{4}{\alpha}-3\right)\lambda(s-t)}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+C\zeta_{1}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega)+5,

uniformly for
\varepsilon\in[0,2]
. Hence, by coming back to (
4.10
) we can deduce that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,2]}\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v }(t,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{t-2}^{t}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t-\varepsilon}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{2}+1\right)\left(e^{-\lambda t}\|v(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\right)+C\int^{0}_{-5}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{6}\right){\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{6}+1\right)\left(e^{-\lambda t}\|v(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\right),\quad t\geq T_{1}(\omega)+5.

In other words, for any
t\geq T_{1}(\omega)+7
and
\eta\in[t-2,t]

(
so that
\eta\geq T_{1}(\omega)+5)
, it can obtain that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{\eta-2}^{\eta}\|A\textbf{v }(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta,\theta_{-\eta-(t-\eta)}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{\eta-2}^{\eta}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-\eta-(t-\eta)}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{6}+1\right)\left(e^{-\lambda\eta}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{6}+1\right)\left(e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\right),

where in the last inequality we have used
\eta\geq t-2
.
Since the initial value
\textbf{v}(0)
comes from within the absorbing set
\mathfrak{B}
, then there exists a random variable
T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega)\geq T_{1}(\omega)+7

such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\textbf{v}(0)\in\mathfrak{B}(\theta_{-t}\omega)}e^{-\lambda t}\|\textbf{v}(0)\|^{2}\leq 1,\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega).

(4.11)

Hence, by (
4.11
), we can get the crucial estimate as follows

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in[t-2,t]}\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\int_{\eta-2}^{\eta}\|A\textbf{v}(s,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{2}\,{\rm{d}}s\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(\sup_{\tau\in(-5,0)}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{6}+1\right)\big(1+\zeta_{1}(\omega)\big)=:\zeta_{3}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega),

(4.12)

where
\zeta_{3}
is a tempered random variable. By (
4.12
), we can deduce that
an immediate consequence is the following estimate

\displaystyle\int_{t-1}^{t}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))\|^{4}\,{\rm{d}}\eta

\displaystyle\leq|\zeta_{3}(\omega)|^{2},\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega).

(4.13)

Inserting (
4.13
) into (
4.1
), we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,w(0))\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{|\zeta_{3}(\omega)|^{2}+C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4}{\rm{d}}\eta}\left(|\zeta_{3}(\omega)|^{2}+\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{s}\omega)|^{4}\,{\rm{d}}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{2|\zeta_{3}(\omega)|^{2}+C\int_{-1}^{0}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4}{\rm{d}}\eta}=:\zeta_{4}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega).

This completes the proof of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.2

(
H^{2}
-distance)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
f\in H
.
There exists a tempered random variable
\zeta_{5}(\cdot)
such that the solutions
v
of the random anisotropic NS equations (
3.17
) and
u
of the deterministic anisotropic NS equations (
3.5
) satisfy

\|A\textbf{v}(t,\theta_{-t}\omega,\textbf{v}(0))-A\textbf{u}(t,\textbf{u}(0))\|^{2}\leq\zeta_{5}(\omega),\quad t\geq T_{\mathfrak{B}}(\omega),

for any
\textbf{v}(0)\in\mathfrak{B}(\theta_{-t}\omega)

and
\textbf{u}(0)\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
,
where
T_{\mathfrak{B}}
denotes the random variable defined in Lemma
4.1
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of the system (
4.1
) with
A^{2}w
in
H
, by integration by parts, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|Aw\|^{2}-\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}w_{1}\Delta^{2}w_{1}dx-\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}w_{2}\Delta^{2}w_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\left(-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),A^{2}w\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))-B(\textbf{u}),A^{2}w\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-\nu A_{1}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),A^{2}w\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(B(\textbf{v}+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)),A^{2}w\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(B(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega),\textbf{u}),A^{2}w\right)

\displaystyle=:I_{4}(t)+I_{5}(t)+I_{6}(t).

(4.14)

By direct computations and
\nabla\cdot w=0
and (
3.16
), it also yields

\displaystyle-\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{yy}\Delta w_{1}\Delta w_{1}dx-\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\partial_{xx}\Delta w_{2}\Delta w_{2}dx

\displaystyle=\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{y}\Delta w_{1})^{2}dx+\nu\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}(\partial_{x}\Delta w_{2})^{2}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\nu\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}.

(4.15)

In order to get (
4.21
), we only to estimate each terms on the right hand side of (
4.1
).
By using Hölder’s inequality, Young’s inequality and
h\in H^{3}
, we can deduce that

\displaystyle I_{4}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\nu\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}.

(4.16)

By using Hölder’s inequality and the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, it also yields

\displaystyle I_{5}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|_{L^{4}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|+\|\textbf{u}_{h}\|_{L^{4}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|_{L^{4}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad+C\|\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\left(\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle=:J_{1}(t)+J_{2}(t).

Since
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is bounded in
H^{2}
,
\|A\textbf{u}(t)\|^{2}+\|A^{1/2}\textbf{u}(t)\|^{2}\leq C
for each
t\geq 0
, hence we can deduce that

\displaystyle\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|Aw\|^{2}+C\|A\textbf{u}\|^{2}+C\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|Aw\|^{2}+C+C\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{2},

(4.17)

and similarly, we also have

\displaystyle\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{2}+C

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|^{2}+C.

(4.18)

Hence, for the term
J_{1}(t)
, we introduce the following inequalities

\displaystyle C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right),

\displaystyle C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{\frac{1}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\|Aw\|^{2}+C\big(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}+1\big)\big(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\big),

\displaystyle C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|Aw\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\left(1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right),

and

\displaystyle C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\|A\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\nu}{64}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}\left(\|Aw\|^{2}+1+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4},

it follows that

\displaystyle J_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{2}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{4}\right)+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}.

For the term
J_{2}(t)
, applying (
4.1
) and the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality and the Young inequality we can deduce that

\displaystyle J_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|\|Aw\|^{\frac{1}{2}}|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\||y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{4}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{8}\right)+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4},

and then for
I_{5}(t)=J_{1}(t)+J_{2}(t)
we can get the following estimate

\displaystyle I_{5}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{8}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{4}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{8}\right)+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}.

(4.19)

For the term
I_{6}(t)
, applying the similar method, we also can deduce that

\displaystyle I_{6}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla\textbf{u}\|_{L^{4}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|+\|w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|A\textbf{u}\|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\quad+C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A\textbf{u}\|\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq C\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A(w+hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega))\|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\left(\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}hy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\right)\big(\|Aw\|+\|Ahy_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)\|\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\nu}{16}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}+C\|Aw\|^{2}+C|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}.

(4.20)

Inserting (
4.16
), (
4.19
) and (
4.1
) into (
4.1
), by (
4.1
) we can get that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm{d}}}{{\rm{d}}t}\|Aw\|^{2}+\frac{\nu}{2}\|A^{\frac{3}{2}}w\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{4}+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}w\|^{2}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{2}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{u}_{h}\|^{8}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{4}\right)\|Aw\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{8}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{t}\omega)|^{8}\right).

(4.21)

For any
t>1
and
s\in[t-1,t]
, applying Gronwall’s lemma to (
4.21
) it is easy to get that

\displaystyle\|Aw(t)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{C\int_{s}^{t}(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}(\eta)\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}\|Aw(s)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{s}^{t}Ce^{C\int_{\tau}^{t}(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{4}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\eta}\omega)|^{4})\,{\rm{d}}\eta}\Big(1+\|A^{\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{v}\|^{8}+|y_{\delta}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)|^{8}\Big)\,{\rm{d}}\tau
